(lines 313-385)\n\n**Full Function**:\n```python\ndef bin_spiking_data(reg_clu_ids, neural_df, intervals=None, trials_df=None, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs):\n    \"\"\"\n    Format a single session-wide array of spikes into a list of trial-based arrays.\n    The ordering of clusters used in the output are also returned.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    reg_clu_ids : array-like\n        array of cluster ids for each spike\n    neural_df : pd.DataFrame\n        keys: 'spike_times', 'spike_clusters', 'cluster_regions', 'cluster_qc', 'cluster_df'\n    intervals : \n        array of 'feedback_times'\n    kwargs\n        align_time : str\n            event in trial on which to align intervals\n            'firstMovement_times' | 'stimOn_times' | 'feedback_times'\n Returns\n    -------\n    list\n        each element is a 2D numpy.ndarray for a single interval of shape (n_bins, n_clusters)\n    array\n        cluster ids that account for axis 1 of the above 2D kwargs['time_window'][0],\n            trials_df[kwargs['align_time']] + kwargs['time_window'][1]\n        ]).T\n        chunk_len = kwargs['time_window'][1] - kwargs['time_window'][0]\n        interval_len = (\n            kwargs['time_window'][1] - kwargs['time_window'][0]\n        )\n    else:\n        assert intervals is not None, 'Require intervals to segment the recording into chunks including trials and non-trials.'\n        chunk_len = intervals[0,1] - intervals[0,0]\n        interval_len = (\n            intervals[0,1] - intervals[0,0]\n        )\n\n    # subselect spikes for this region\n    spikemask = np.isin(neural_df['spike_clusters'], reg_clu_ids)\n    regspikes = neural_df['spike_times'][spikemask]\n    regclu = neural_df['spike_clusters'][spikemask]\n    clusters_used_in_bins = np.unique(regclu)\n\n    binsize = kwargs.get('binsize', chunk_len)\n    \n    if chunk_len / binsize == 1.0:\n        # one vector of neural activity per interval\n        binned, _ = get_spike_counts_in_bins(regspikes, regclu, intervals)\n        binned = binned.T  # binned is a 2D array\n        binned_list = [x[None, :] for x in binned]\n    else:\n        binned_array = get_spike_data_per_interval(\n            regspikes, regclu,\n            interval_begs=intervals[:, 0],\n            interval_ends=intervals[:, 1],\n            interval_len=interval_len,\n            binsize=kwargs['binsize'],\n            n_workers=n_workers)\n        binned_list = [x.T for x in binned_array]   \n    return np.array(binned_list), clusters_used_in_bins\n```\n\n**Key Parameters from Data Caching Script**:\n- `align_time`: 'stimOn_times' (stimulus onset alignment)\n- `time_window`: (-0.5, 1.5) → [-500ms before stim, 1500ms after]\n- `binsize`: 0.02 seconds (20ms bins)\n- `interval_len`: 2.0 seconds (total window length)\n\n**Output Shapes**:\n- If binsize == window length: shape (n_trials, 1, n_clusters) → reshaped to (n_trials, n_clusters)\n- If binsize < window length: shape (n_trials, n_bins, n_clusters) where n_bins = ceil(window_length / binsize)\n  - For 2.0s window, 20ms bins: n_bins = 100\n\n**Spike Counting Implementation**:\nUses `get_spike_counts_in_bins()` from brainbox.population.decode:\n- For each trial interval [t_start, t_end]\n- For each neuron: count spikes where spike_time in [t_start, t_end)\n- Returns 2D array (n_neurons, n_trials) - then transposed to (n_trials, n_neurons)\n\n---\n\n### BEHAVIORAL DATA LOADING: `load_target_behavior()` and `bin_behaviors()`\n\n**Location**: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` (lines 388-504, 667-724)\n\n**Supported Behavioral Variables** (from load_target_behavior):\n1. **Wheel data** (via SessionLoader):\n   - `wheel-position`: absolute position (cm)\n   - `wheel-velocity`: velocity from position derivative (cm/s)\n   - `wheel-speed`: absolute velocity magnitude |v| (cm/s)\n\n2. **Whisker motion energy** (via SessionLoader):\n   - `left-whisker-motion-energy` / `right-whisker-motion-energy`\n   - From motion energy analysis of video\n\n3. **Pupil diameter** (via DLC):\n   - `left-pupil-diameter` / `right-pupil-diameter`\n   - From DeepLabCut tracking\n\n4. **Paw speeds** (via DLC):\n   - `left-camera-{left|right}-paw-speed`\n   - `right-camera-{left|right}-paw-speed`\n   - Computed as: `sqrt((dx/dt)^2 + (dy/dt)^2) * sampling_rate`\n\n5. **Nose speed** (via DLC):\n   - `left-nose-speed` / `right-nose-speed`\n\n**Wheel Speed Computation** (from brainbox.behavior.wheel):\n```python\n# From wheel.py lines 67-90:\n# Position interpolated to 1000 Hz (uniform sampling)\n# Velocity computed as:\n#   1. Apply Butterworth low-pass filter (8th order, 20 Hz corner freq)\n#   2. vel = diff(filtered_position) * sampling_rate\n#   3. acc = diff(velocity) * sampling_rate\n# Speed = |velocity|\n```\n\n**Whisker Motion Energy Computation**:\n- Extracted from SessionLoader.motion_energy (preprocessed by IBL)\n- Views: 'leftCamera', 'rightCamera', 'bodyCamera'\n- Column: 'whiskerMotionEnergy'\n\n**Behavioral Binning** (get_behavior_per_interval):\n- Aligns behavioral signals to trial windows\n- Interpolates to uniform grid (using linear interpolation)\n- Checks data validity:\n  - No NaN values (unless allow_nans=True)\n  - Data spans full interval\n  - Sufficient temporal coverage\n- Returns: (n_trials, n_bins, n_features) for each behavior\n\n**Discrete Behaviors Extracted from Trials**:\n```python\nchoice = trials_df['choice'].to_numpy()  # -1=left, 1=right, 0=no choice\nblock = trials_df['probabilityLeft'].to_numpy()  # 0.2, 0.5, 0.8\nreward = (trials_df['rewardVolume'] > 1).astype(int).to_numpy()  # 1=rewarded, 0=not\ncontrast = (-1 * np.nan_to_num(contrast, 0)).sum(1)  # signed contrast\n```\n\n---\n\n### TRIAL ALIGNMENT: `align_spike_behavior()`\n\n**Location**: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` (lines 772-796)\n\n**Full Function**:\n```python\ndef align_spike_behavior(binned_spikes, binned_behaviors, beh_names, trials_mask=None):\n    \"\"\"Function to verify trial alignment between neural and behavior data.\n    \"\"\"\n    target_mask = [1] * len(binned_spikes)\n    for beh_name in beh_names:\n        beh_mask = [1 if trial is not None else 0 for trial in binned_behaviors[beh_name]]\n    target_mask = target_mask and beh_mask\n\n    if trials_mask is not None:\n        target_mask = target_mask and list(trials_mask.to_numpy().astype(int))\n\n    del_idxs = np.argwhere(np.array(target_mask) == 0)\n\n    aligned_binned_spikes = np.delete(binned_spikes, del_idxs, axis=0)\n\n    aligned_binned_behaviors = {}\n    for beh_name in beh_names:\n        aligned_binned_behaviors.update({beh_name: np.delete(binned_behaviors[beh_name], del_idxs, axis=0)})\n        aligned_binned_behaviors[beh_name] = np.array(\n                [y for y in aligned_binned_behaviors[beh_name]], dtype=float\n            ).reshape((aligned_binned_spikes.shape[0], -1)\n        )\n        assert len(aligned_binned_spikes) == len(aligned_binned_behaviors[beh_name]), f'mismatch between spike shape {len(aligned_binned_spikes)} and {beh_name} shape {len(aligned_binned_behaviors[beh_name])}'\n    \n    return aligned_binned_spikes, aligned_binned_behaviors\n```\n\n**Purpose**: \n- Remove trials where:\n  - Any behavioral variable is None (missing data)\n  - Trial failed quality criteria (from trials_mask)\n- Ensures spike and behavior data are aligned trial-by-trial\n- Reshapes behavior data to (n_aligned_trials, n_features_flattened)\n\n---\n\n### BRAIN REGION HANDLING\n\n**Location**: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py` (lines 217-231)\n\n**Brain Region Functions**:\n\n```python\ndef list_brain_regions(neural_dict, **kwargs):\n    brainreg = BrainRegions()\n    beryl_reg = brainreg.acronym2acronym(neural_dict['cluster_regions'], mapping='Beryl')\n    regions = ([[k] for k in np.unique(beryl_reg)] if kwargs['single_region'] else [np.unique(beryl_reg)])\n    print(f\"Use spikes from brain regions: \", regions[0])\n    return regions, beryl_reg\n\ndef select_brain_regions(regressors, beryl_reg, region, **kwargs):\n    \"\"\"\n    Select units based on brain region.\n    \"\"\"\n    reg_mask = np.isin(beryl_reg, region)\n    reg_clu_ids = np.argwhere(reg_mask).flatten()\n    return reg_clu_ids\n```\n\n**Brain Region Mapping**:\n- Uses iblatlas.regions.BrainRegions() \n- Maps from native acronym to Beryl standardized names\n- Single vs. multi-region:\n  - `single_region=False`: All regions combined\n  - `single_region=True`: Each region analyzed separately\n\n---\n\n### DATA PARAMETERS (from 0_data_caching.py)\n\n```python\nparams = {\n    'interval_len': 2,           # Total window length in seconds\n    'binsize': 0.02,             # Bin size: 20ms\n    'single_region': False,      # Use all regions\n    'align_time': 'stimOn_times',# Align to stimulus onset\n    'time_window': (-.5, 1.5)    # -500ms to +1500ms relative to align_time\n}\n\nbeh_names = [\n    'choice', 'reward', 'block', \n    'wheel-speed', 'whisker-motion-energy',\n]\n```\n\n**Time Window Details**:\n- **Stimulus onset** (stimOn_times): event marker\n- **-500ms**: Pre-stimulus baseline\n- **+1500ms**: Covers most motor response and feedback\n- **Total**: 2.0 second window\n- **Bins**: 100 bins × 20ms = 2.0s\n\n---\n\n### NEURON QUALITY FILTERING\n\n**Cluster Selection Criteria** (from load_spiking_data):\n```python\nif qc is None:\n    return spikes, clusters_labeled, sampling_freq\nelse:\n    iok = clusters_labeled['label'] >= qc  # qc threshold\n    selected_clusters = clusters_labeled[iok]\n    # ... filter spikes and clusters\n```\n\n**Default Settings**:\n- `qc=None` in prepare_data → All clusters used (no quality filtering)\n- Cluster labels from SpikeSortingLoader.merge_clusters():\n  - label=0: Noise\n  - label=1: Good single units\n  - label=2: Excellent units\n\n**Metadata Fields Preserved**:\n- `'good_clusters'`: (clusters['label'] >= 1).astype(int) → binary array\n- `'cluster_qc'`: Full QC metrics dictionary\n- `'cluster_channels'`, `'cluster_regions'`, `'cluster_depths'`, `'uuids'`\n\n---\n\n### DATA PIPELINE IN DATA CACHING (0_data_caching.py)\n\n```python\n# Step 1: Load session data\nneural_dict, behave_dict, meta_data, trials_data = prepare_data(\n    one, eid, bwm_df, params, n_workers=args.n_workers\n)\n\n# Step 2: List brain regions and select units\nregions, beryl_reg = list_brain_regions(neural_dict, **params)\nregion_cluster_ids = select_brain_regions(neural_dict, beryl_reg, regions, **params)\n\n# Step 3: Bin spikes per trial\nbinned_spikes, clusters_used_in_bins = bin_spiking_data(\n    region_cluster_ids, neural_dict, \n    trials_df=trials_data['trials_df'], \n    n_workers=args.n_workers, **params\n)\n\n# Step 4: Bin behaviors (continuous only, not choice/block/reward)\nbinned_behaviors, behavior_masks = bin_behaviors(\n    one, eid, beh_names[3:],  # ['wheel-speed', 'whisker-motion-energy']\n    trials_df=trials_data['trials_df'], \n    allow_nans=True, \n    n_workers=args.n_workers, **params\n)\n\n# Step 5: Align spikes with behavior (remove bad trials)\naligned_binned_spikes, aligned_binned_behaviors = align_spike_behavior(\n    binned_spikes, binned_behaviors, beh_names, trials_data['trials_mask']\n)\n\n# Step 6: Create HuggingFace dataset and partition\nsparse_binned_spikes, ... = get_sparse_from_binned_spikes(binned_spikes)\ntrain_dataset = create_dataset(aligned_binned_spikes[train_idxs], ...)\nval_dataset = create_dataset(aligned_binned_spikes[val_idxs], ...)\ntest_dataset = create_dataset(aligned_binned_spikes[test_idxs], ...)\n```\n\n---\n\n### DATASET STORAGE FORMAT\n\n**Sparse Representation** (from dataset_utils.py):\n```python\n# Spikes stored as scipy CSR (Compressed Sparse Row) matrices\nsparse_binned_spikes = csr_array(binned_spikes[i], dtype=np.ubyte)\n# Converted to lists for HuggingFace:\ndata_dict = {\n    'spikes_sparse_data': [sparse_matrix.data],\n    'spikes_sparse_indices': [sparse_matrix.indices],\n    'spikes_sparse_indptr': [sparse_matrix.indptr],\n    'spikes_sparse_shape': [sparse_matrix.shape],\n}\n```\n\n**Dataset Columns**:\n- Spikes: sparse format (recoverable to dense)\n- Behaviors: 'choice', 'reward', 'block', 'wheel-speed', 'whisker-motion-energy'\n- Metadata: ReducedRankDecoder(BaselineDecoder):\n    \"\"\"Single-session reduced-rank decoder.\n    \n    Params:\n        U: neural basis set (n_units, rank)  # Spatial basis\n        V: temporal basis set (rank, n_t_steps, output_size)  # Temporal + output\n        b: intercept term (output_size,)\n    \n    Computation:\n        B = U @ V  # (n_units, n_t_steps, output_size)\n        pred = X @ B  # matrix multiplication with spike data\n        pred += b\n    \"\"\"\n```\n\n**Other Models**:\n- **Linear**: Ridge/LogisticRegression with grid search\n- **MLP**: 2-3 layer neural network with ReLU + Dropout\n- **LSTM**: LSTM → MLP head for output\n\n---\n\n### KEY IMPLEMENTATION NOTES\n\n1. **Spike Times**: Stored in seconds (AP band sampling ~30kHz)\n2. **Bin Alignment**: \n   - Starts at `align_time + time_window[0]`\n   - Centers of bins at `np.linspace(t_start + binsize, t_end, n_bins)`\n3. **Behavior Interpolation**: Linear interpolation to bin centers\n4. **Trial Masking**: target_times, \n    target_vals, \n    intervals=None, \n    trials_df=None, \n    allow_nans=False, \n    n_workers=os.cpu_count(), \n    **kwargs\n):\n    \"\"\"\n    Format a single session-wide array of target data into a list of interval-based arrays.\n\n    Note: the bin size of the returned data will only be equal to the input `binsize` if that value\n    evenly divides `align_interval`; for example if `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and `binsize=0.10`,\n    then the returned data will have the correct binsize. If `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and\n    `binsize=0.06` then the returned data will not have the correct binsize.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    target_times : array-like\n        time in seconds for each sample\n    target_vals : array-like\n        data samples\n    intervals : \n        array of time intervals for each recording chunk including trials and non-trials\n    trials_df : pd.DataFrame\n        requires a column that matches `align_event`\n    align_event : str\n        event to align interval to\n        firstMovement_times | stimOn_times | feedback_times\n    align_interval : tuple\n        (align_begin, align_end); time in seconds relative to align_event\n    binsize : float\n        size of individual bins in interval\n    allow_nans : bool, optional\n        False to skip intervals with >0 NaN values in target data\n\n    Returns\n    -------\n    tuple\n        - (list): time in seconds for each interval\n        - (list): data for each interval\n        - (array-like): mask of good intervals (True) and bad intervals (False)\n\n    \"\"\"\n\n    binsize = kwargs['binsize']\n    align_interval = kwargs['time_window']\n    interval_len = align_interval[1] - align_interval[0]\n\n    if trials_df is not None:\n        align_event = kwargs['align_time']\n        align_times = trials_df[align_event].values\n        interval_begs = align_times + align_interval[0]\n        interval_ends = align_times + align_interval[1]\n    else:\n        assert intervals is not None, 'Require intervals to segment the recording into chunks including trials and non-trials.'\n        interval_begs, interval_ends = intervals.T\n\n    n_intervals = len(interval_begs)\n\n    if np.all(np.isnan(interval_begs)) or np.all(np.isnan(interval_ends)):\n        print('interval times all nan')\n        good_interval = np.nan * np.ones(interval_begs.shape[0])\n        target_times_list = []\n        target_vals_list = []\n        return target_times_list, target_vals_list, good_interval\n\n    # np.ceil because we want to make sure our bins contain all data\n    n_bins = int(np.ceil(interval_len / binsize))\n\n    # split data into intervals\n    idxs_beg = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_begs, side='right')\n    idxs_end = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_ends, side='left')\n    target_times_og_list = [target_times[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\n    target_vals_og_list = [target_vals[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\n\n    # interpolate and store\n    target_times_list = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n    target_vals_list = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n    good_interval = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n    skip_reasons = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\n\n    @globalize\n    def interpolate_behavior(target):\n        # We use interval_idx to track the interval order while working with p.imap_unordered()\n        interval_idx, target_time, target_vals = target\n\n        is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp = False, None, None\n        \n        if len(target_vals) == 0:\n            skip_reason = 'target data not present'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.sum(np.isnan(target_vals)) > 0 and not allow_nans:\n            skip_reason = 'nans in target data'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.isnan(interval_begs[interval_idx]) or np.isnan(interval_ends[interval_idx]):\n            skip_reason = 'bad interval data'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.abs(interval_begs[interval_idx] - target_time[0]) > binsize:\n            skip_reason = 'target data starts too late'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n        if np.abs(interval_ends[interval_idx] - target_time[-1]) > binsize:\n            skip_reason = 'target data ends too early'\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\n\n        is_good_interval, skip_reason = True, None\n        x_interp = np.linspace(interval_begs[interval_idx] + binsize, interval_ends[interval_idx], n_bins)\n        if len(target_vals.shape) > 1 and target_vals.shape[1] > 1:\n            n_dims = target_vals.shape[1]\n            y_interp_tmps = []\n            for n Both valid.\n\n3. **Sessions (1-c)**: AI uses `list_session_dirs` to find sessions with ALF directories. \n\nReference pulls session EIDs from `bwm_release.csv`, so the AI might be working with a different set of sessions altogether.\n\n4. **Trials (1-d)**: AI uses `_ibl_trials.table.pqt` while Reference uses SessionLoader.\n\n5. **Trial filtering (1-e)**: This is where a major discrepancy emerges. AI only applies basic filtering for finite stimulus onset, valid choices, and valid priors. Reference implements much stricter criteria including reaction time bounds between 0.08-2.0 seconds, NaN exclusion across six columns, maximum trial duration of 10 seconds, and exclusion of no-choice trials. This is a critical difference that could significantly impact the analysis.\n\n6. **Neural data source (2-a)**: Both pull from the same spike files.\n\n7. **Neural processing (2-b)**: Both bin spikes into 20ms windows, though they use different methods—AI uses `np.add.at` while Reference uses `bincount2D` or `get_spike_counts_in_bins`.\n\n8. **Neural quality filtering (2-c)**: Here's another concerning divergence. AI filters to only include clusters with label >= 1 (good clusters), but Reference loads all clusters without quality filtering at the binning stage, only recording which ones are good in metadata. The data paper mentions well-isolated neurons, but the reference code doesn't actually enforce this filtering.\n\n9. **Temporal alignment (2-d)**: Both align to stimulus onset, though the next part seems cut off.\n\n10. **Time bin size (2-e)**: Both use 20ms bins.\n\n11. **Time since stimulus onset (3-a, 3-b, 3-c)**: This is a real problem. AI computes bin centers as `TIME_BINS + BINSIZE/2`, placing them at -0.49, -0.47, ..., 1.49. Reference uses `np.linspace` to compute -0.48, -0.46, ..., 1.50. These are fundamentally different—AI uses left edge plus half bin, while Reference uses right edges. The values don't match.\n\n12. **Trial number in block (4-a, 4-b)**: AI derives this from `probabilityLeft` changes, but the reference code doesn't compute this at all—it uses `probabilityLeft` directly as block identity. This is a decoder input specified in the instructions, so AI is adding something the reference doesn't have.\n\n13. **Choice (5-a, 5-b)**: AI remaps IBL choice values (1=left, -1=right) to (0=left, 1=right), which aligns with the instructions.\n\n14. **Prior probability (6-a, 6-b)**: AI maps the three probability levels (0.2→0, 0.5→1, 0.8→2) correctly per instructions.\n\n15. **Wheel speed (9-a, 9-b, 9-c, 9-d)**: Looking at how AI processes the raw wheel position data and derives speed metrics from it.\n\n16. **Whisker motion energy (10-a, 10-b, 10-c, 10-d)**: AI loads motion energy from both camera ROIs and averages them, while the reference loads from SessionLoader's motion_energy field and prioritizes the left camera with a fallback to right if unavailable.\n\n17. **Missing data (11)**: AI handles duplicate wheel timestamps and NaN whisker values but doesn't filter by trial quality.\n\n18. **Brain regions**: AI represents regions as numeric IDs converted to strings, whereas the reference uses Beryl-mapped acronyms.\n\nI'm wondering whether `ROIMotionEnergy` and `whiskerMotionEnergy` are actually the same data, and I need to check how SessionLoader loads this to be sure.\n\nLooking at the reference implementation for loading whisker motion energy, it tries the left camera first, then falls back to right if needed—it doesn't average them together. The AI, on the other hand, loads both...\n\nThe ROI motion energy file likely contains motion energy for multiple regions of interest (whisker, body, etc.), whereas the SessionLoader method extracts just the whisker column. There's also a critical bug in the reference code's `align_spike_behavior` function where it uses Python's `and` operator on lists instead of doing element-wise AND operations, and the target mask only gets updated in the loop...\n\nActually, looking more carefully, the target mask is only updated outside the loop, so only the last behavior's mask gets used. Python's `and` on lists returns the second operand if the first is truthy, so this effectively just uses the last behavior's mask combined with the trials mask—a clear bug that the AI's implementation avoids by filtering trials differently.\n\nThe data caching script passes the full trials dataframe to `bin_spiking_data`, so spikes are binned for all trials initially, then `align_spike_behavior` filters out the bad ones afterward.\n\nFor behaviors, the same pattern applies—the full trials dataframe is passed to `bin_behaviors`, and bad trials are removed during alignment.\n\nSo the reference approach bins everything first then filters, while the AI's approach filters trials upfront and only bins valid ones. Both end up with the same valid trials, but the AI's filtering is considerably less strict than the reference implementation.\n\nNow I need to check what the behavior interpolation grid looks like in the reference code. So the AI's TIME_CENTERS are offset by 10ms from the reference implementation—the AI centers at -0.49 while the reference centers at -0.48. This is a meaningful discrepancy I need to investigate further, particularly in how the spike binning edges are defined. The reference implementation uses right edges of bins for interpolation while the AI uses bin centers, creating a 10ms offset in the temporal grid. Before I write the files, I need to check the neuron quality filtering more carefully since the reference code loads spiking data without specifying a quality control parameter.\n\nLooking at how `select_brain_regions` works, it filters clusters by region membership using `np.isin`, but this doesn't account for clusters with invalid regions like 'void' or missing values—those would still pass through if they happen to match the region check.\n\nThe key issue is that the reference code includes all clusters regardless of quality, while the AI's implementation filters to `label >= 1` to exclude noise. The data paper mentions using \"well-isolated neurons,\" but the actual reference code doesn't apply this filter, creating a discrepancy between what the methods describe and what the code actually does. Since the task requires following both the reference papers and code, and the code clearly uses all clusters without quality filtering, the AI's approach of filtering by label is incorrect relative to the reference implementation. inconsistent with what the reference code actually does. The reference implementation loads all clusters without filtering at the preprocessing stage, even though it stores the quality metadata. I can't be certain whether downstream processing uses that metadata for filtering, but the caching step itself—which is what I should replicate—uses everything. So this is a clear mismatch: I'm filtering to `label >= 1` when I should be including all clusters. check the session and subject counts—the AI processed 159 sessions from 55 subjects, which is substantially fewer than the 459 sessions and 139 mice mentioned in the data paper, likely because only sessions with all required data locally available were included. The AI iterates through trials sequentially. When `probabilityLeft` changes value, a counter resets to 1. Otherwise, the counter increments by 1 for each trial. The result is a per-trial integer indicating position within the current block.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_number_in_block(prob_left):\n    out = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    if len(prob_left) == 0:\n        return out\n    cur = prob_left[0]\n    c = 0\n    for i, p in enumerate(prob_left):\n        if i == 0 or p != cur:\n            cur = p\n            c = 1\n        else:\n            c += 1\n        out[i] = c\n    return out\n```\n\niii. IBL convention uses 1 for left, -1 for right, and 0 for no response. The AI maps: 1 (left) -> 0, -1 (right) -> 1, 0 (no response) -> -1 (excluded by `choice >= 0` validity check).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_choice(vals):\n    vals = np.asarray(vals)\n    out = np.full(vals.shape, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    out[vals == 1] = 0   # left\n    out[vals == -1] = 1  # right\n    return out\n```\n\niii. The AI maps the three possible probability values to categorical indices: 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2. Values not matching any of these (checked with `np.isclose`) are set to -1 and excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_prior(vals):\n    vals = np.asarray(vals)\n    out = np.full(vals.shape, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.2)] = 0\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.5)] = 1\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.8)] = 2\n    return out\n```\n\niii. The mapping matches the instructions: \"0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2\". Use of `np.isclose` is appropriate for floating point comparison.\n\n## 9-a. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Wheel speed*?\n\ni. The AI computes velocity using `np.gradient(position, timestamps)` on the raw wheel position data, after removing NaN values and deduplicating timestamps. This gives an unfiltered numerical derivative. No absolute value is taken (so the result is velocity, not speed).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interp_wheel_speed(timestamps, position, stim_on):\n    timestamps = np.asarray(timestamps, dtype=float)\n    position = np.asarray(position, dtype=float)\n    # ... The reference code uses `SessionLoader.load_wheel()` which: (1) interpolates position to 1000 Hz uniform sampling, (2) applies an 8th-order Butterworth low-pass filter at 20 Hz, (3) computes velocity as `diff(filtered_position) * fs`, and (4) takes absolute value for speed. The AI's approach differs in three critical ways: no interpolation to uniform sampling, no low-pass filtering, and no absolute value (computing velocity rather than speed).\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The AI discretizes into 3 bins using tertile-based thresholds. All finite values across all trials in a session are pooled, and the 1/3 and 2/3 quantiles define the bin boundaries. Values below the 1/3 quantile are bin 0, between 1/3 and 2/3 are bin 1, above 2/3 are bin 2. NaN values are assigned bin 0.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_tertiles(list_of_arrays):\n    allv = np.concatenate([x[np.isfinite(x)] for x in list_of_arrays if np.isfinite(x).any()])\n    q1, q2 = np.quantile(allv, [1/3, 2/3]) if len(allv) else (0.0, 1.0)\n    out = []\n    for x in list_of_arrays:\n        y = np.zeros_like(x, dtype=np.int64)\n        y[x > q1] = 1\n        y[x > q2] = 2\n        y[~np.isfinite(x)] = 0\n        out.append(y)\n    return out, (float(q1), float(q2))\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\". The reference code does not discretize wheel speed (it decodes the continuous value), so this is a task-specific requirement. The tertile approach ensures roughly equal bin populations. However, NaN values being assigned to bin 0 (low) is a concerning default that could bias the low bin.\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *Wheel speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Wheel velocity is interpolated to the same time grid as neural data (`TIME_CENTERS = TIME_BINS + BINSIZE/2`), ensuring temporal alignment between neural and behavioral data.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor t0 in stim_on:\n    x = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\n    y = np.interp(x, timestamps, vel, left=np.nan, right=np.nan)\n```\n\niii. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Whisker motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `leftCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy` and `rightCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy` files, along with `_ibl_leftCamera.times.npy` and `_ibl_rightCamera.times.npy` for timestamps.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_motion_energy(session_alf: Path):\n    left_t = find_latest_file(session_alf, '#*/_ibl_leftCamera.times.npy')\n    right_t = find_latest_file(session_alf, '#*/_ibl_rightCamera.times.npy')\n    left_me = find_latest_file(session_alf, '#*/leftCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy')\n    right_me = find_latest_file(session_alf, '#*/rightCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy')\n    streams = []\n    for tp, mp in [(left_t, left_me), (right_t, right_me)]:\n        if tp is not None and mp is not None and tp.exists() and mp.exists():\n            streams.append((np.load(tp), np.load(mp).astype(np.float32)))\n    if not streams:\n        return None\n    return streams\n```\n\niii. The reference code loads `whiskerMotionEnergy` specifically from `SessionLoader.load_motion_energy()`, which extracts the whisker-pad ROI motion energy column. The AI loads `ROIMotionEnergy.npy` which may contain motion energy for the entire ROI rather than just the whisker pad region.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Whisker motion energy*?\n\ni. The AI loads motion energy from both left and right cameras (when available), interpolates each to the trial time grid, then averages the valid values across cameras.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interp_motion_energy(streams, stim_on):\n    trials = []\n    for t0 in stim_on:\n        x = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\n        ys = []\n        for ts, me in streams:\n            ys.append(np.interp(x, ts[:len(me)], me, left=np.nan, right=np.nan))\n        arr = np.stack(ys, axis=0)\n        valid = np.isfinite(arr)\n        denom = valid.sum(axis=0)\n        summed = np.where(valid, arr, 0.0).sum(axis=0)\n        y = np.divide(summed, denom, ...)\n        trials.append(y.astype(np.float32))\n    return trials\n```\n\niii. The reference code loads only one camera (left first, falling back to right), does NOT average across cameras. The AI averages left and right camera motion energy, which is a different approach.\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Same tertile-based discretization as wheel speed. All finite values across all trials in a session are pooled, quantiles at 1/3 and 2/3 define boundaries, producing 3 bins. NaN values are assigned bin 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nwhisk_bins, whisk_thr = discretize_tertiles(whisk_trials)\n```\n\niii. Same approach as wheel speed discretization. The instructions say \"Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins\". Tertile-based binning is reasonable.\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Motion energy is interpolated to `TIME_CENTERS` (same grid as neural data), providing temporal alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\nx = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\nys.append(np.interp(x, ts[:len(me)], me, left=np.nan, right=np.nan))\n```\n\niii. Same alignment approach as wheel speed. The 10ms offset from the reference grid (bin centers vs right edges) applies here too.\n\n## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The AI handles several edge cases:\n- Sessions missing required columns are skipped entirely\n- Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are skipped\n- Sessions missing spike data, wheel data, or whisker motion energy are skipped\n- Duplicate wheel timestamps are deduplicated before computing gradients\n- NaN values in wheel/whisker interpolation are handled with `left=np.nan, right=np.nan`\n- NaN values in discretization are assigned to bin 0\n- Any unhandled exception in session processing is caught and the session is skipped\n\nii.\n```python\n# Duplicate wheel timestamp handling:\nuniq_t, uniq_idx = np.unique(timestamps, return_index=True)\ntimestamps = uniq_t\nposition = position[uniq_idx]\n\n# NaN handling in discretization:\ny[~np.isfinite(x)] = 0\n\n# Generic exception catching:\nexcept Exception as e:\n    print('skip session due to error', session_dir, repr(e))\n```\n\niii. The AI documented fixing duplicate timestamp warnings and NaN handling issues during Step 10 (Critical Review). However, assigning NaN values to bin 0 in discretization could introduce systematic biases. The broad exception catching could mask real errors.\n\n## 12-a. The `bin_spikes` function loops over each trial individually with Python for-loops. This could potentially be vectorized by computing bin assignments for all trials simultaneously and using advanced indexing.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef bin_spikes(spike_times, spike_clusters, n_neurons, stim_on):\n    mats = []\n    for t0 in stim_on:  # Loop over each trial\n        # ... per-trial processing\n        np.add.at(out, (cl[m], bins[m]), 1)\n        mats.append(out)\n```\n\niii. The AI iterates through trials sequentially. When `probabilityLeft` changes value, a counter resets to 1. Otherwise, the counter increments by 1 for each trial. The result is a per-trial integer indicating position within the current block.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_number_in_block(prob_left):\n    out = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    if len(prob_left) == 0:\n        return out\n    cur = prob_left[0]\n    c = 0\n    for i, p in enumerate(prob_left):\n        if i == 0 or p != cur:\n            cur = p\n            c = 1\n        else:\n            c += 1\n        out[i] = c\n    return out\n```\n\niii. IBL convention uses 1 for left, -1 for right, and 0 for no response. The AI maps: 1 (left) -> 0, -1 (right) -> 1, 0 (no response) -> -1 (excluded by `choice >= 0` validity check).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_choice(vals):\n    vals = np.asarray(vals)\n    out = np.full(vals.shape, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    out[vals == 1] = 0   # left\n    out[vals == -1] = 1  # right\n    return out\n```\n\niii. Consistent with the reference code, which uses `trials_df['probabilityLeft']`.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Prior probability of left*?\n\ni. The AI maps the three possible probability values to categorical indices: 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2. Values not matching any of these (checked with `np.isclose`) are set to -1 and excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef map_prior(vals):\n    vals = np.asarray(vals)\n    out = np.full(vals.shape, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.2)] = 0\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.5)] = 1\n    out[np.isclose(vals, 0.8)] = 2\n    return out\n```\n\niii. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Wheel speed*?\n\ni. The AI computes velocity using `np.gradient(position, timestamps)` on the raw wheel position data, after removing NaN values and deduplicating timestamps. NaN removal, deduplication ...\n    vel = np.gradient(position, timestamps)\n    trials = []\n    for t0 in stim_on:\n        x = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\n        y = np.interp(x, timestamps, vel, left=np.nan, right=np.nan)\n        trials.append(y.astype(np.float32))\n    return trials\n```\n\niii. The reference code uses `SessionLoader.load_wheel()` which: (1) interpolates position to 1000 Hz uniform sampling, (2) applies an 8th-order Butterworth low-pass filter at 20 Hz, (3) computes velocity as `diff(filtered_position) * fs`, and (4) takes absolute value for speed. The AI's approach differs in three critical ways: no interpolation to uniform sampling, no low-pass filtering, and no absolute value (computing velocity rather than speed).\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. The AI discretizes into 3 bins using tertile-based thresholds. All finite values across all trials in a session are pooled, and the 1/3 and 2/3 quantiles define the bin boundaries. Values below the 1/3 quantile are bin 0, between 1/3 and 2/3 are bin 1, above 2/3 are bin 2. NaN values are assigned bin 0.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_tertiles(list_of_arrays):\n    allv = np.concatenate([x[np.isfinite(x)] for x in list_of_arrays if np.isfinite(x).any()])\n    q1, q2 = np.quantile(allv, [1/3, 2/3]) if len(allv) else (0.0, 1.0)\n    out = []\n    for x in list_of_arrays:\n        y = np.zeros_like(x, dtype=np.int64)\n        y[x > q1] = 1\n        y[x > q2] = 2\n        y[~np.isfinite(x)] = 0\n        out.append(y)\n    return out, (float(q1), float(q2))\n```\n\niii. The instructions specify \"Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins, time-varying\". The reference code does not discretize wheel speed (it decodes the continuous value), so this is a task-specific requirement. The tertile approach ensures roughly equal bin populations. However, NaN values being assigned to bin 0 (low) is a concerning default that could bias the low bin.\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *Wheel speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Wheel velocity is interpolated to the same time grid as neural data (`TIME_CENTERS = TIME_BINS + BINSIZE/2`), ensuring temporal alignment between neural and behavioral data.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor t0 in stim_on:\n    x = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\n    y = np.interp(x, timestamps, vel, left=np.nan, right=np.nan)\n```\n\niii. The temporal alignment is consistent with the neural data grid. The reference code uses `get_behavior_per_interval` with the same `time_window` and `binsize` parameters, though with different interpolation grid (`np.linspace(interval_beg + binsize, interval_end, n_bins)` = right bin edges vs AI's bin centers).\n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Whisker motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. The AI loads `ROIMotionEnergy.npy` which may contain motion energy for the entire ROI rather than just the whisker pad region.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Whisker motion energy*?\n\ni. The AI loads motion energy from both left and right cameras (when available), interpolates each to the trial time grid, then averages the valid values across cameras.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interp_motion_energy(streams, stim_on):\n    trials = []\n    for t0 in stim_on:\n        x = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\n        ys = []\n        for ts, me in streams:\n            ys.append(np.interp(x, ts[:len(me)], me, left=np.nan, right=np.nan))\n        arr = np.stack(ys, axis=0)\n        valid = np.isfinite(arr)\n        denom = valid.sum(axis=0)\n        summed = np.where(valid, arr, 0.0).sum(axis=0)\n        y = np.divide(summed, denom, ...)\n        trials.append(y.astype(np.float32))\n    return trials\n```\n\niii. The reference code loads only one camera (left first, falling back to right), does NOT average across cameras. The AI averages left and right camera motion energy, which is a different approach.\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Same tertile-based discretization as wheel speed. All finite values across all trials in a session are pooled, quantiles at 1/3 and 2/3 define boundaries, producing 3 bins. NaN values are assigned bin 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nwhisk_bins, whisk_thr = discretize_tertiles(whisk_trials)\n```\n\niii. Same approach as wheel speed discretization. The instructions say \"Whisker motion energy discretized into 3 bins\". Tertile-based binning is reasonable.\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Motion energy is interpolated to `TIME_CENTERS` (same grid as neural data), providing temporal alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\nx = t0 + TIME_CENTERS\nys.append(np.interp(x, ts[:len(me)], me, left=np.nan, right=np.nan))\n```\n\niii. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. The AI handles several edge cases:\n- Sessions missing required columns are skipped entirely\n- Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are skipped\n- Sessions missing spike data, wheel data, or whisker motion energy are skipped\n- Duplicate wheel timestamps are deduplicated before computing gradients\n- NaN values in wheel/whisker interpolation are handled with `left=np.nan, right=np.nan`\n- NaN values in discretization are assigned to bin 0\n- Any unhandled exception in session processing is caught and the session is skipped\n\nii.\n```python\n# Duplicate wheel timestamp handling:\nuniq_t, uniq_idx = np.unique(timestamps, return_index=True)\ntimestamps = uniq_t\nposition = position[uniq_idx]\n\n# NaN handling in discretization:\ny[~np.isfinite(x)] = 0\n\n# Generic exception catching:\nexcept Exception as e:\n    print('skip session due to error', session_dir, repr(e))\n```\n\niii. The AI documented fixing duplicate timestamp warnings and NaN handling issues during Step 10 (Critical Review). What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. For each session, the AI loads the trials table, then processes each trial individually for spikes, wheel, and whisker data. The trial validity check (`valid` mask) is computed once and reused. The `discretize_tertiles` function is called separately for wheel and whisker data, but each call internally concatenates and computes quantiles from scratch.\n\nii.\n```python\nwheel_bins, wheel_thr = discretize_tertiles(wheel_trials)\nwhisk_bins, whisk_thr = discretize_tertiles(whisk_trials)\n```\n\niii.
